Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, doc, it's... it's a lot, to be honest. My wife actually pushed me to come, but I know I need to be here. I've been an electrician for, gosh, over twenty years now. Started right after I did a couple years of college and realized sitting at a desk just wasn't for me. I know wires, you know? I know circuits, I know loads, I know how to make things safe. That's my whole job, making sure things are safe and done by the book. 

But... I came in today because I just can't shake this thought, this heavy, sinking feeling in my gut that just won't go away. It's just... if all my years of experience, all that training as an electrician couldn't keep my own brother safe... then what good am I? I mean, I really believe I'm a danger to anybody who works with me on a job site, or even the people who live under my own roof. If I couldn't protect him with all the knowledge I have, how can I be trusted around anyone else? Every time I pick up my tools or even look at the breaker box at home, my mind just goes right to it, thinking I'm going to ruin someone else's life. It's just this constant loop in my head that I'm a hazard, a walking hazard, and I don't know how to turn it off or how to even be around my family when I feel like I'm just going to end up getting them hurt.
=====================================

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I... I don't... doc, I just can't. I mean, I try to put it into words, you know? My wife, she asks me to talk about it, she sits there on the edge of the bed with that look, that really patient, loving look, and I open my mouth and... it's like the words just evaporate. I can tell you about the morning, I can tell you we had coffee from that little gas station down on Route 9, I can tell you the sun was just barely coming up and my truck was running rough because I hadn't changed the spark plugs... it was just a Tuesday, a stupid, completely normal Tuesday. We were just supposed to be doing a standard job. 

But when it comes to the actual... to the moment it went wrong... I just... my brain hits a wall. It's like a circuit breaker tripping, ironically enough. Just... *snap*, and everything goes dark and loud at the same time. I want to tell you, I know I'm supposed to tell you so you can figure this out and help me, but I literally can't make the words come out of my throat. It's just this massive knot of noise and... and guilt, just so much guilt. I just know I should have stopped it. I should have known better, I was the one with the training. But the actual thing that happened? The details of it? I can't... I can't even say it out loud. I've tried, I really have. I'll sit in my truck for hours after work just trying to say it to the steering wheel and I just... I can't do it. I just can't.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, yeah. Yeah, I do. It's... it's gotten pretty bad, honestly. Take my own garage, for instance. I used to spend hours out there. I had a whole setup, you know? Workbench, pegboard with all my tools outlined so I knew exactly where everything was, a little mini-fridge for a couple beers on a Saturday afternoon. It was my sanctuary. But now? Now I don't go in there unless I absolutely, positively have to. Like, if the lawnmower is in there and the grass is a foot high and my wife is giving me that look, I'll go in, grab it, and get out as fast as I can. Just being in there, surrounded by all those tools, the smell of oil and sawdust... it just makes my chest tight. I can't breathe right.

And it's not just the garage. I used to volunteer down at our church, you know? Just helping out with the electrical stuff. They've got this old building, beautiful but the wiring is a nightmare, a real patchwork job from the seventies. I was slowly going through, updating the panels, making sure it was up to code so, God forbid, nothing happened. But I had to quit. I just... I called the pastor and told him I didn't have the time anymore, which was a lie, but I couldn't face it. I couldn't look at another breaker box that wasn't for my actual paying job, and even those are hard enough. 

And people... man, people don't get it. They mean well, but they just don't get it. Guys at work or folks around the neighborhood, they'll start talking about, I don't know, restoring an old car, or they'll bring up... they'll bring up the fire. Just casually, like it's the weather or last night's game. And I just shut them down. Fast. I don't even try to be polite about it anymore. I'll just turn my back and walk away, or I'll change the subject so hard they get whiplash. I just can't hear about it. I can't hear about car projects, I can't hear about anything that even skirts around the edges of... of what happened. It's like if I let them talk, if I let myself be around any of those things, that whole knot of noise in my head is just going to explode. So, yeah, I avoid a lot. I avoid pretty much everything I can.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah. Yeah, all the time. It's... it's not even like a memory, doc, it's not like when you remember what you had for breakfast or, you know, a vacation you took years ago. It's... it's like I'm time-traveling, but not the fun kind you see in the movies. It's like my brain just yanks me backward. 

I'll just be at work, right? Middle of a Tuesday, perfectly normal day. I'll be in the middle of wiring a panel, stripping the casing off a 12-gauge wire, my hands doing what they've done ten thousand times before, and then... I don't know, maybe a truck backfires out on the street, or maybe someone drops a heavy wrench on the concrete. Or even at home, my nephew, he'll come over and he'll slam the heavy metal garage door shut, you know the way kids do, just yank it down so it hits the floor hard... and instantly, I'm not there anymore. The whole job site, or my driveway, it just drops right out from under me. 

Suddenly, I'm not holding my wire strippers. I'm right back in that moment. I can smell it. God, the smell of the smoke, it's so thick and acrid, it burns the back of my throat, like burning plastic and oil and... and I can see him. I can see my brother. I can see the whole thing happening right in front of me, in real time, and my heart starts hammering against my ribs so hard I think I'm going to have a heart attack right then and there. I'll start sweating, my hands will shake, and I'll actually yell out, like I'm trying to warn him, but I'm standing in an unfinished kitchen in a new subdivision, yelling at a drywaller who's staring at me like I've lost my mind. It takes me... I don't know, minutes, sometimes longer, just to realize where I actually am, to feel the floor under my boots again. It's terrifying, doc. It's like I'm never really safe from it, because it can just ambush me out of nowhere.

Like, if the lawnmower is in there and the grass is a foot high and my wife is giving me that look, I'll go in, grab it, and get out as fast as I can. Just being in there, surrounded by all those tools, the smell of oil and sawdust... it just makes my chest tight. I can't breathe right.\n\nAnd it's not just the garage. I used to volunteer down at our church, you know? Just helping out with the electrical stuff. They've got this old building, beautiful but the wiring is a nightmare, a real patchwork job from the seventies. I was slowly going through, updating the panels, making sure it was up to code so, God forbid, nothing happened. But I had to quit. I just... I called the pastor and told him I didn't have the time anymore, which was a lie, but I couldn't face it. I couldn't look at another breaker box that wasn't for my actual paying job, and even those are hard enough. \n\nAnd people... man, people don't get it. They mean well, but they just don't get it. Guys at work or folks around the neighborhood, they'll start talking about, I don't know, restoring an old car, or they'll bring up... they'll bring up the fire. Just casually, like it's the weather or last night's game. And I just shut them down. Fast. I don't even try to be polite about it anymore. I'll just turn my back and walk away, or I'll change the subject so hard they get whiplash. I just can't hear about it. I can't hear about car projects, I can't hear about anything that even skirts around the edges of... of what happened. It's like if I let them talk, if I let myself be around any of those things, that whole knot of noise in my head is just going to explode. So, yeah, I avoid a lot. I avoid pretty much everything I can.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever experience memories of what happened to your brother that feel like they're happening all over again, like you're reliving them?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Yeah, all the time. It's... it's not even like a memory, doc, it's not like when you remember what you had for breakfast or, you know, a vacation you took years ago. It's... it's like I'm time-traveling, but not the fun kind you see in the movies. It's like my brain just yanks me backward. \n\nI'll just be at work, right? Middle of a Tuesday, perfectly normal day. I'll be in the middle of wiring a panel, stripping the casing off a 12-gauge wire, my hands doing what they've done ten thousand times before, and then... I don't know, maybe a truck backfires out on the street, or maybe someone drops a heavy wrench on the concrete. Or even at home, my nephew, he'll come over and he'll slam the heavy metal garage door shut, you know the way kids do, just yank it down so it hits the floor hard... and instantly, I'm not there anymore. The whole job site, or my driveway, it just drops right out from under me. \n\nSuddenly, I'm not holding my wire strippers. I'm right back in that moment. I can smell it. God, the smell of the smoke, it's so thick and acrid, it burns the back of my throat, like burning plastic and oil and... and I can see him. I can see my brother. I can see the whole thing happening right in front of me, in real time, and my heart starts hammering against my ribs so hard I think I'm going to have a heart attack right then and there. I'll start sweating, my hands will shake, and I'll actually yell out, like I'm trying to warn him, but I'm standing in an unfinished kitchen in a new subdivision, yelling at a drywaller who's staring at me like I've lost my mind. It takes me... I don't know, minutes, sometimes longer, just to realize where I actually am, to feel the floor under my boots again. It's terrifying, doc. It's like I'm never really safe from it, because it can just ambush me out of nowhere.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It's like I'm never really safe from it, because it can just ambush me out of nowhere.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel guilty about what happened to your brother?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 42 / electrician / some_college\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State\nmanifestation: \"There's this heavy mix of guilt and anger sitting in my chest all day, especially when I look at my nephew and think he lost his dad and got stuck with me instead.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Guilt? I... doc, guilt doesn't even begin to cover it. It's not just a passing thought, it's like a physical thing. There's this heavy, suffocating mix of guilt and anger just sitting right here in the middle of my chest, all day, every single day. From the minute I wake up and stare at the ceiling, before I even put my work boots on, it's there. I'll be driving to a job site, gripping the steering wheel, and I'm just so angry at myself, angry at how everything went down, and the guilt is just eating me alive from the inside out. 

But it's the worst... God, it's the absolute worst when I look at my nephew. My brother's boy. He's over at the house a lot now, you know, because my sister-in-law is picking up extra shifts to keep the mortgage paid, so my wife and I watch him. And he's a great kid, he really is, he's got his dad's exact same smile, that same way of squinting when he's trying to figure out how a tool works. But every time I look at him across the kitchen table while he's doing his math homework, or when he asks me to help him fix the chain on his bike... it just destroys me. 

I look at this kid and all I can think is that he lost his dad. He lost his actual dad, the guy who was supposed to teach him to drive, the guy who was supposed to be there for everything. And instead? Instead he got stuck with me. He's stuck with the uncle who was supposed to protect his dad, the guy with the electrical training who should have stopped the whole thing from happening. 

It's just so incredibly unfair to him, you know? I'm sitting there trying to be a surrogate father figure, trying to smile and be normal, and my chest is just screaming with this anger and this guilt. I'm angry my brother is gone, I'm angry that I'm the one who walked away, and I just feel so sick with guilt that this poor kid has to settle for me. I can't shake it, doc. It never lifts. It just sits there, heavy and dark, all day long. I don't know how to live with it.

I've been an electrician for, gosh, over twenty years now. Started right after I did a couple years of college and realized sitting at a desk just wasn't for me. I know wires, you know? I know circuits, I know loads, I know how to make things safe. That's my whole job, making sure things are safe and done by the book. \n\nBut... I came in today because I just can't shake this thought, this heavy, sinking feeling in my gut that just won't go away. It's just... if all my years of experience, all that training as an electrician couldn't keep my own brother safe... then what good am I? I mean, I really believe I'm a danger to anybody who works with me on a job site, or even the people who live under my own roof. If I couldn't protect him with all the knowledge I have, how can I be trusted around anyone else? Every time I pick up my tools or even look at the breaker box at home, my mind just goes right to it, thinking I'm going to ruin someone else's life. It's just this constant loop in my head that I'm a hazard, a walking hazard, and I don't know how to turn it off or how to even be around my family when I feel like I'm just going to end up getting them hurt.\n\nDoctor: What happened to your brother?\n\nPatient: I... I don't... doc, I just can't. I mean, I try to put it into words, you know? My wife, she asks me to talk about it, she sits there on the edge of the bed with that look, that really patient, loving look, and I open my mouth and... it's like the words just evaporate. I can tell you about the morning, I can tell you we had coffee from that little gas station down on Route 9, I can tell you the sun was just barely coming up and my truck was running rough because I hadn't changed the spark plugs... it was just a Tuesday, a stupid, completely normal Tuesday. We were just supposed to be doing a standard job. \n\nBut when it comes to the actual... to the moment it went wrong... I just... my brain hits a wall. It's like a circuit breaker tripping, ironically enough. Just... *snap*, and everything goes dark and loud at the same time. I want to tell you, I know I'm supposed to tell you so you can figure this out and help me, but I literally can't make the words come out of my throat. It's just this massive knot of noise and... and guilt, just so much guilt. I just know I should have stopped it. I should have known better, I was the one with the training. But the actual thing that happened? The details of it? I can't... I can't even say it out loud. I've tried, I really have. I'll sit in my truck for hours after work just trying to say it to the steering wheel and I just... I can't do it. I just can't.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding places, people, or activities that remind you of what happened to your brother?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah. Yeah, I do. It's... it's gotten pretty bad, honestly. Take my own garage, for instance. I used to spend hours out there. I had a whole setup, you know? Workbench, pegboard with all my tools outlined so I knew exactly where everything was, a little mini-fridge for a couple beers on a Saturday afternoon. It was my sanctuary. But now? Now I don't go in there unless I absolutely, positively have to. Like, if the lawnmower is in there and the grass is a foot high and my wife is giving me that look, I'll go in, grab it, and get out as fast as I can. Just being in there, surrounded by all those tools, the smell of oil and sawdust... it just makes my chest tight. I can't breathe right.\n\nAnd it's not just the garage. I used to volunteer down at our church, you know? Just helping out with the electrical stuff. They've got this old building, beautiful but the wiring is a nightmare, a real patchwork job from the seventies. I was slowly going through, updating the panels, making sure it was up to code so, God forbid, nothing happened. But I had to quit. I just... I called the pastor and told him I didn't have the time anymore, which was a lie, but I couldn't face it. I couldn't look at another breaker box that wasn't for my actual paying job, and even those are hard enough. \n\nAnd people... man, people don't get it. They mean well, but they just don't get it. Guys at work or folks around the neighborhood, they'll start talking about, I don't know, restoring an old car, or they'll bring up... they'll bring up the fire. Just casually, like it's the weather or last night's game. And I just shut them down. Fast. I don't even try to be polite about it anymore. I'll just turn my back and walk away, or I'll change the subject so hard they get whiplash. I just can't hear about it. I can't hear about car projects, I can't hear about anything that even skirts around the edges of... of what happened. It's like if I let them talk, if I let myself be around any of those things, that whole knot of noise in my head is just going to explode. So, yeah, I avoid a lot. I avoid pretty much everything I can.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever experience memories of what happened to your brother that feel like they're happening all over again, like you're reliving them?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Yeah, all the time. It's... it's not even like a memory, doc, it's not like when you remember what you had for breakfast or, you know, a vacation you took years ago. It's... it's like I'm time-traveling, but not the fun kind you see in the movies. It's like my brain just yanks me backward. \n\nI'll just be at work, right? Middle of a Tuesday, perfectly normal day. I'll be in the middle of wiring a panel, stripping the casing off a 12-gauge wire, my hands doing what they've done ten thousand times before, and then... I don't know, maybe a truck backfires out on the street, or maybe someone drops a heavy wrench on the concrete. Or even at home, my nephew, he'll come over and he'll slam the heavy metal garage door shut, you know the way kids do, just yank it down so it hits the floor hard... and instantly, I'm not there anymore. The whole job site, or my driveway, it just drops right out from under me. \n\nSuddenly, I'm not holding my wire strippers. I'm right back in that moment. I can smell it. God, the smell of the smoke, it's so thick and acrid, it burns the back of my throat, like burning plastic and oil and... and I can see him. I can see my brother. I can see the whole thing happening right in front of me, in real time, and my heart starts hammering against my ribs so hard I think I'm going to have a heart attack right then and there. I called the pastor and told him I didn't have the time anymore, which was a lie, but I couldn't face it. I couldn't look at another breaker box that wasn't for my actual paying job, and even those are hard enough. \n\nAnd people... man, people don't get it. They mean well, but they just don't get it. Guys at work or folks around the neighborhood, they'll start talking about, I don't know, restoring an old car, or they'll bring up... they'll bring up the fire. Just casually, like it's the weather or last night's game. And I just shut them down. Fast. I don't even try to be polite about it anymore. I'll just turn my back and walk away, or I'll change the subject so hard they get whiplash. I just can't hear about it. I can't hear about car projects, I can't hear about anything that even skirts around the edges of... of what happened. It's like if I let them talk, if I let myself be around any of those things, that whole knot of noise in my head is just going to explode. So, yeah, I avoid a lot. I avoid pretty much everything I can.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever experience memories of what happened to your brother that feel like they're happening all over again, like you're reliving them?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Yeah, all the time. It's... it's not even like a memory, doc, it's not like when you remember what you had for breakfast or, you know, a vacation you took years ago. It's... it's like I'm time-traveling, but not the fun kind you see in the movies. It's like my brain just yanks me backward. \n\nI'll just be at work, right? Middle of a Tuesday, perfectly normal day. I'll be in the middle of wiring a panel, stripping the casing off a 12-gauge wire, my hands doing what they've done ten thousand times before, and then... I don't know, maybe a truck backfires out on the street, or maybe someone drops a heavy wrench on the concrete. Or even at home, my nephew, he'll come over and he'll slam the heavy metal garage door shut, you know the way kids do, just yank it down so it hits the floor hard... and instantly, I'm not there anymore. The whole job site, or my driveway, it just drops right out from under me. \n\nSuddenly, I'm not holding my wire strippers. I'm right back in that moment. I can smell it. God, the smell of the smoke, it's so thick and acrid, it burns the back of my throat, like burning plastic and oil and... and I can see him. I can see my brother. I can see the whole thing happening right in front of me, in real time, and my heart starts hammering against my ribs so hard I think I'm going to have a heart attack right then and there. I'll start sweating, my hands will shake, and I'll actually yell out, like I'm trying to warn him, but I'm standing in an unfinished kitchen in a new subdivision, yelling at a drywaller who's staring at me like I've lost my mind. It takes me... I don't know, minutes, sometimes longer, just to realize where I actually am, to feel the floor under my boots again. It's terrifying, doc. It's like I'm never really safe from it, because it can just ambush me out of nowhere.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about what happened to your brother?\n\nPatient: Guilt? I... doc, guilt doesn't even begin to cover it. It's not just a passing thought, it's like a physical thing. There's this heavy, suffocating mix of guilt and anger just sitting right here in the middle of my chest, all day, every single day. From the minute I wake up and stare at the ceiling, before I even put my work boots on, it's there. I'll be driving to a job site, gripping the steering wheel, and I'm just so angry at myself, angry at how everything went down, and the guilt is just eating me alive from the inside out. \n\nBut it's the worst... God, it's the absolute worst when I look at my nephew. My brother's boy. He's over at the house a lot now, you know, because my sister-in-law is picking up extra shifts to keep the mortgage paid, so my wife and I watch him. And he's a great kid, he really is, he's got his dad's exact same smile, that same way of squinting when he's trying to figure out how a tool works. But every time I look at him across the kitchen table while he's doing his math homework, or when he asks me to help him fix the chain on his bike... it just destroys me. \n\nI look at this kid and all I can think is that he lost his dad. He lost his actual dad, the guy who was supposed to teach him to drive, the guy who was supposed to be there for everything. And instead? Instead he got stuck with me. He's stuck with the uncle who was supposed to protect his dad, the guy with the electrical training who should have stopped the whole thing from happening. \n\nIt's just so incredibly unfair to him, you know? I'm sitting there trying to be a surrogate father figure, trying to smile and be normal, and my chest is just screaming with this anger and this guilt. I'm angry my brother is gone, I'm angry that I'm the one who walked away, and I just feel so sick with guilt that this poor kid has to settle for me. I can't shake it, doc. It never lifts. It just sits there, heavy and dark, all day long. I don't know how to live with it.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever thought about harming yourself since your brother's accident?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Harming myself... well... I mean, I haven't, like, sat down and written a note or planned anything out, if that's what you're asking. I don't have a specific plan to do anything. But... I guess, if I'm being completely honest with you, doc, I haven't exactly been keeping myself safe, either. It's... it's hard to explain. It's more like I just don't care anymore. 

Like, I've been driving way, way too fast between jobs lately. I'm usually in my heavy work truck, the bed is just loaded down with heavy tools and spools of wire, and I'll be out on the highway just burying the needle, going 90, 95 miles an hour, weaving in and out of lanes. I know it's incredibly stupid. Before all this, I was the guy who always drove the speed limit, the guy who always checked his blind spots twice because I wanted to get home to my wife. But now? Now I just press the gas pedal down to the floorboard, I watch the cars blur by, and I just don't feel anything. It's like I'm daring the road to take me out. 

And it's not just the driving, it's at work, too. As an electrician, safety is literally the first thing they teach you on day one of your apprenticeship. Lock out, tag out, always assume a circuit is live until you test it yourself with your meter. You respect the electricity or it kills you, it's that simple. But lately... man, I'll be working around live wires, main service panels with enough juice to stop your heart in a fraction of a second, and I just don't slow down like I used to. I don't double-check the breakers. I'll just reach right in there, stripping wires and making connections hot, completely ignoring every single safety rule I've followed for the last twenty years. My hands are right there next to the bare copper, and I know exactly what would happen if my pliers slipped or if I grounded out, but I just... I don't care. 

It's almost like I don't care if something bad happens to me. If I wrap the truck around a bridge abutment on my way to a site, or if I take a lethal shock in some stranger's basement... part of me thinks that would just be it, you know? It would just be over. The guilt, the noise in my head, the waking up every day feeling like I'm suffocating... it would all just stop. So, no, I'm not actively holding a gun to my head, but I'm definitely living like a guy who wouldn't mind if the lights went out for good.

I know circuits, I know loads, I know how to make things safe. That's my whole job, making sure things are safe and done by the book. \n\nBut... Now I don't go in there unless I absolutely, positively have to. Like, if the lawnmower is in there and the grass is a foot high and my wife is giving me that look, I'll go in, grab it, and get out as fast as I can. Just being in there, surrounded by all those tools, the smell of oil and sawdust... it just makes my chest tight. I can't breathe right.\n\nAnd it's not just the garage. I couldn't look at another breaker box that wasn't for my actual paying job, and even those are hard enough. \n\nAnd people... man, people don't get it. They mean well, but they just don't get it. Just casually, like it's the weather or last night's game. And I just shut them down. Fast. I don't even try to be polite about it anymore. I'll just turn my back and walk away, or I'll change the subject so hard they get whiplash. I just can't hear about it. I can't hear about car projects, I can't hear about anything that even skirts around the edges of... of what happened. It's... it's like I'm time-traveling, but not the fun kind you see in the movies. It's like my brain just yanks me backward. \n\nI'll just be at work, right? Middle of a Tuesday, perfectly normal day. I'll be in the middle of wiring a panel, stripping the casing off a 12-gauge wire, my hands doing what they've done ten thousand times before, and then... I don't know, maybe a truck backfires out on the street, or maybe someone drops a heavy wrench on the concrete. Or even at home, my nephew, he'll come over and he'll slam the heavy metal garage door shut, you know the way kids do, just yank it down so it hits the floor hard... and instantly, I'm not there anymore. The whole job site, or my driveway, it just drops right out from under me. \n\nSuddenly, I'm not holding my wire strippers. I'm right back in that moment. I can smell it. God, the smell of the smoke, it's so thick and acrid, it burns the back of my throat, like burning plastic and oil and... and I can see him. I can see my brother. I can see the whole thing happening right in front of me, in real time, and my heart starts hammering against my ribs so hard I think I'm going to have a heart attack right then and there. I'll start sweating, my hands will shake, and I'll actually yell out, like I'm trying to warn him, but I'm standing in an unfinished kitchen in a new subdivision, yelling at a drywaller who's staring at me like I've lost my mind. It takes me... I don't know, minutes, sometimes longer, just to realize where I actually am, to feel the floor under my boots again. It's terrifying, doc. It's like I'm never really safe from it, because it can just ambush me out of nowhere.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about what happened to your brother?\n\nPatient: Guilt? I... doc, guilt doesn't even begin to cover it. It's not just a passing thought, it's like a physical thing. There's this heavy, suffocating mix of guilt and anger just sitting right here in the middle of my chest, all day, every single day. From the minute I wake up and stare at the ceiling, before I even put my work boots on, it's there. I'll be driving to a job site, gripping the steering wheel, and I'm just so angry at myself, angry at how everything went down, and the guilt is just eating me alive from the inside out. \n\nBut it's the worst... God, it's the absolute worst when I look at my nephew. My brother's boy. He's over at the house a lot now, you know, because my sister-in-law is picking up extra shifts to keep the mortgage paid, so my wife and I watch him. And he's a great kid, he really is, he's got his dad's exact same smile, that same way of squinting when he's trying to figure out how a tool works. But every time I look at him across the kitchen table while he's doing his math homework, or when he asks me to help him fix the chain on his bike... it just destroys me. \n\nI look at this kid and all I can think is that he lost his dad. He lost his actual dad, the guy who was supposed to teach him to drive, the guy who was supposed to be there for everything. And instead? Instead he got stuck with me. He's stuck with the uncle who was supposed to protect his dad, the guy with the electrical training who should have stopped the whole thing from happening. \n\nIt's just so incredibly unfair to him, you know? I'm sitting there trying to be a surrogate father figure, trying to smile and be normal, and my chest is just screaming with this anger and this guilt. I'm angry my brother is gone, I'm angry that I'm the one who walked away, and I just feel so sick with guilt that this poor kid has to settle for me. I can't shake it, doc. It never lifts. It just sits there, heavy and dark, all day long. I don't know how to live with it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about harming yourself since your brother's accident?\n\nPatient: Harming myself... well... I mean, I haven't, like, sat down and written a note or planned anything out, if that's what you're asking. I don't have a specific plan to do anything. But... I guess, if I'm being completely honest with you, doc, I haven't exactly been keeping myself safe, either. It's... it's hard to explain. It's more like I just don't care anymore. \n\nLike, I've been driving way, way too fast between jobs lately. I'm usually in my heavy work truck, the bed is just loaded down with heavy tools and spools of wire, and I'll be out on the highway just burying the needle, going 90, 95 miles an hour, weaving in and out of lanes. I know it's incredibly stupid. Before all this, I was the guy who always drove the speed limit, the guy who always checked his blind spots twice because I wanted to get home to my wife. But now? Now I just press the gas pedal down to the floorboard, I watch the cars blur by, and I just don't feel anything. It's like I'm daring the road to take me out. \n\nAnd it's not just the driving, it's at work, too. As an electrician, safety is literally the first thing they teach you on day one of your apprenticeship. Lock out, tag out, always assume a circuit is live until you test it yourself with your meter. You respect the electricity or it kills you, it's that simple. I don't double-check the breakers. I'll just reach right in there, stripping wires and making connections hot, completely ignoring every single safety rule I've followed for the last twenty years. My hands are right there next to the bare copper, and I know exactly what would happen if my pliers slipped or if I grounded out, but I just... I don't care. \n\nIt's almost like I don't care if something bad happens to me. It's more like I just don't care anymore. \n\nLike, I've been driving way, way too fast between jobs lately. I'm usually in my heavy work truck, the bed is just loaded down with heavy tools and spools of wire, and I'll be out on the highway just burying the needle, going 90, 95 miles an hour, weaving in and out of lanes. I know it's incredibly stupid. Before all this, I was the guy who always drove the speed limit, the guy who always checked his blind spots twice because I wanted to get home to my wife. But now? Now I just press the gas pedal down to the floorboard, I watch the cars blur by, and I just don't feel anything. It's like I'm daring the road to take me out. \n\nAnd it's not just the driving, it's at work, too. As an electrician, safety is literally the first thing they teach you on day one of your apprenticeship. Lock out, tag out, always assume a circuit is live until you test it yourself with your meter. You respect the electricity or it kills you, it's that simple. But lately... man, I'll be working around live wires, main service panels with enough juice to stop your heart in a fraction of a second, and I just don't slow down like I used to. I don't double-check the breakers. I'll just reach right in there, stripping wires and making connections hot, completely ignoring every single safety rule I've followed for the last twenty years. My hands are right there next to the bare copper, and I know exactly what would happen if my pliers slipped or if I grounded out, but I just... I don't care. \n\nIt's almost like I don't care if something bad happens to me. If I wrap the truck around a bridge abutment on my way to a site, or if I take a lethal shock in some stranger's basement... part of me thinks that would just be it, you know? It would just be over. The guilt, the noise in my head, the waking up every day feeling like I'm suffocating... it would all just stop. So, no, I'm not actively holding a gun to my head, but I'm definitely living like a guy who wouldn't mind if the lights went out for good.\n\n[Last Question]\nHow long have you been experiencing these feelings of guilt and anxiety since your brother's accident?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 42 / electrician / some_college\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Duration\nmanifestation: \"This has been going on for about four months, ever since the garage fire and my nephew coming to live with us.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Four months. It's been about four months, doc. Ever since... well, ever since the garage fire. Four months doesn't sound like a long time when you just say it out loud, you know? Like, if you're talking about a commercial job, four months is nothing, you're barely past the framing and the electrical rough-in. But living with this? Every single day of these last four months has felt like a goddamn eternity. 

It started right after the fire, obviously, but it really just... it locked in, it got so much heavier when my nephew started staying with us. Because, like I was saying before, my sister-in-law had to figure out what to do to keep them afloat. The funeral expenses, the mortgage, all of it just dropped right on her shoulders overnight, so she started taking all those extra shifts. And suddenly the boy is just... he's at our house all the time. He's practically living with us. Every afternoon, every weekend. 

So for four solid months, I wake up, I have this crushing weight of guilt sitting right on my chest, and then I walk out into my kitchen and there he is. Just sitting there eating cereal, looking exactly like his dad. And I can't escape it. I can't even just go home and try to clear my head after work because the living, breathing reminder of what I let happen is sitting right there at my kitchen table. Four months of just... suffocating. I keep thinking maybe one morning I'll wake up and it won't be so loud in my head, but it's just been one long, continuous nightmare since the day that garage burned. Honestly, I don't know how people live with this kind of thing for years. Just these four months alone feel like they've aged me ten years.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, it's... it's a lot, to be honest. My wife actually pushed me to come, but I know I need to be here. I've been an electrician for, gosh, over twenty years now. Started right after I did a couple years of college and realized sitting at a desk just wasn't for me. I know wires, you know? I know circuits, I know loads, I know how to make things safe. That's my whole job, making sure things are safe and done by the book. \n\nBut... I came in today because I just can't shake this thought, this heavy, sinking feeling in my gut that just won't go away. It's just... if all my years of experience, all that training as an electrician couldn't keep my own brother safe... then what good am I? I mean, I really believe I'm a danger to anybody who works with me on a job site, or even the people who live under my own roof. If I couldn't protect him with all the knowledge I have, how can I be trusted around anyone else? Every time I pick up my tools or even look at the breaker box at home, my mind just goes right to it, thinking I'm going to ruin someone else's life. It's just this constant loop in my head that I'm a hazard, a walking hazard, and I don't know how to turn it off or how to even be around my family when I feel like I'm just going to end up getting them hurt.\n\nDoctor: What happened to your brother?\n\nPatient: I... I don't... doc, I just can't. I mean, I try to put it into words, you know? My wife, she asks me to talk about it, she sits there on the edge of the bed with that look, that really patient, loving look, and I open my mouth and... it's like the words just evaporate. I can tell you about the morning, I can tell you we had coffee from that little gas station down on Route 9, I can tell you the sun was just barely coming up and my truck was running rough because I hadn't changed the spark plugs... it was just a Tuesday, a stupid, completely normal Tuesday. We were just supposed to be doing a standard job. \n\nBut when it comes to the actual... to the moment it went wrong... I just... my brain hits a wall. It's like a circuit breaker tripping, ironically enough. Just... *snap*, and everything goes dark and loud at the same time. I want to tell you, I know I'm supposed to tell you so you can figure this out and help me, but I literally can't make the words come out of my throat. It's just this massive knot of noise and... and guilt, just so much guilt. I just know I should have stopped it. I should have known better, I was the one with the training. But the actual thing that happened? The details of it? I can't... I can't even say it out loud. I've tried, I really have. I'll sit in my truck for hours after work just trying to say it to the steering wheel and I just... I can't do it. I just can't.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding places, people, or activities that remind you of what happened to your brother?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah. Yeah, I do. It's... it's gotten pretty bad, honestly. Take my own garage, for instance. I used to spend hours out there. I had a whole setup, you know? Workbench, pegboard with all my tools outlined so I knew exactly where everything was, a little mini-fridge for a couple beers on a Saturday afternoon. It was my sanctuary. But now? Now I don't go in there unless I absolutely, positively have to. Like, if the lawnmower is in there and the grass is a foot high and my wife is giving me that look, I'll go in, grab it, and get out as fast as I can. Just being in there, surrounded by all those tools, the smell of oil and sawdust... it just makes my chest tight. I can't breathe right.\n\nAnd it's not just the garage. I used to volunteer down at our church, you know? Just helping out with the electrical stuff. They've got this old building, beautiful but the wiring is a nightmare, a real patchwork job from the seventies. I was slowly going through, updating the panels, making sure it was up to code so, God forbid, nothing happened. But I had to quit. I just... I called the pastor and told him I didn't have the time anymore, which was a lie, but I couldn't face it. I couldn't look at another breaker box that wasn't for my actual paying job, and even those are hard enough. \n\nAnd people... man, people don't get it. They mean well, but they just don't get it. Guys at work or folks around the neighborhood, they'll start talking about, I don't know, restoring an old car, or they'll bring up... they'll bring up the fire. Just casually, like it's the weather or last night's game. And I just shut them down. Fast. I don't even try to be polite about it anymore. I'll just turn my back and walk away, or I'll change the subject so hard they get whiplash. I just can't hear about it. I can't hear about car projects, I can't hear about anything that even skirts around the edges of... of what happened. It's like if I let them talk, if I let myself be around any of those things, that whole knot of noise in my head is just going to explode. So, yeah, I avoid a lot. I avoid pretty much everything I can.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever experience memories of what happened to your brother that feel like they're happening all over again, like you're reliving them?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Yeah, all the time. It's... it's not even like a memory, doc, it's not like when you remember what you had for breakfast or, you know, a vacation you took years ago. It's... it's like I'm time-traveling, but not the fun kind you see in the movies. It's like my brain just yanks me backward. \n\nI'll just be at work, right? Middle of a Tuesday, perfectly normal day. I'll be in the middle of wiring a panel, stripping the casing off a 12-gauge wire, my hands doing what they've done ten thousand times before, and then... I don't know, maybe a truck backfires out on the street, or maybe someone drops a heavy wrench on the concrete. Or even at home, my nephew, he'll come over and he'll slam the heavy metal garage door shut, you know the way kids do, just yank it down so it hits the floor hard... and instantly, I'm not there anymore. The whole job site, or my driveway, it just drops right out from under me. \n\nSuddenly, I'm not holding my wire strippers. I'm right back in that moment. I can smell it. God, the smell of the smoke, it's so thick and acrid, it burns the back of my throat, like burning plastic and oil and... and I can see him. I can see my brother. I can see the whole thing happening right in front of me, in real time, and my heart starts hammering against my ribs so hard I think I'm going to have a heart attack right then and there. I'll start sweating, my hands will shake, and I'll actually yell out, like I'm trying to warn him, but I'm standing in an unfinished kitchen in a new subdivision, yelling at a drywaller who's staring at me like I've lost my mind. It takes me... I don't know, minutes, sometimes longer, just to realize where I actually am, to feel the floor under my boots again. It's terrifying, doc. It's like I'm never really safe from it, because it can just ambush me out of nowhere.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about what happened to your brother?\n\nPatient: Guilt? I... doc, guilt doesn't even begin to cover it. It's not just a passing thought, it's like a physical thing. There's this heavy, suffocating mix of guilt and anger just sitting right here in the middle of my chest, all day, every single day. From the minute I wake up and stare at the ceiling, before I even put my work boots on, it's there. I'll be driving to a job site, gripping the steering wheel, and I'm just so angry at myself, angry at how everything went down, and the guilt is just eating me alive from the inside out. \n\nBut it's the worst... God, it's the absolute worst when I look at my nephew. My brother's boy. He's over at the house a lot now, you know, because my sister-in-law is picking up extra shifts to keep the mortgage paid, so my wife and I watch him. And he's a great kid, he really is, he's got his dad's exact same smile, that same way of squinting when he's trying to figure out how a tool works. But every time I look at him across the kitchen table while he's doing his math homework, or when he asks me to help him fix the chain on his bike... it just destroys me. \n\nI look at this kid and all I can think is that he lost his dad. He lost his actual dad, the guy who was supposed to teach him to drive, the guy who was supposed to be there for everything. And instead? Instead he got stuck with me. He's stuck with the uncle who was supposed to protect his dad, the guy with the electrical training who should have stopped the whole thing from happening. \n\nIt's just so incredibly unfair to him, you know? I'm sitting there trying to be a surrogate father figure, trying to smile and be normal, and my chest is just screaming with this anger and this guilt. I'm angry my brother is gone, I'm angry that I'm the one who walked away, and I just feel so sick with guilt that this poor kid has to settle for me. I can't shake it, doc. It never lifts. It just sits there, heavy and dark, all day long. I don't know how to live with it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about harming yourself since your brother's accident?\n\nPatient: Harming myself... well... I'm usually in my heavy work truck, the bed is just loaded down with heavy tools and spools of wire, and I'll be out on the highway just burying the needle, going 90, 95 miles an hour, weaving in and out of lanes. I know it's incredibly stupid. Before all this, I was the guy who always drove the speed limit, the guy who always checked his blind spots twice because I wanted to get home to my wife. But now? Now I just press the gas pedal down to the floorboard, I watch the cars blur by, and I just don't feel anything. It's like I'm daring the road to take me out. \n\nAnd it's not just the driving, it's at work, too. As an electrician, safety is literally the first thing they teach you on day one of your apprenticeship. Lock out, tag out, always assume a circuit is live until you test it yourself with your meter. You respect the electricity or it kills you, it's that simple. But lately... man, I'll be working around live wires, main service panels with enough juice to stop your heart in a fraction of a second, and I just don't slow down like I used to. I don't double-check the breakers. I'll just reach right in there, stripping wires and making connections hot, completely ignoring every single safety rule I've followed for the last twenty years. My hands are right there next to the bare copper, and I know exactly what would happen if my pliers slipped or if I grounded out, but I just... I don't care. \n\nIt's almost like I don't care if something bad happens to me. If I wrap the truck around a bridge abutment on my way to a site, or if I take a lethal shock in some stranger's basement... part of me thinks that would just be it, you know? It would just be over. The guilt, the noise in my head, the waking up every day feeling like I'm suffocating... it would all just stop. So, no, I'm not actively holding a gun to my head, but I'm definitely living like a guy who wouldn't mind if the lights went out for good.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these feelings of guilt and anxiety since your brother's accident?\n\nPatient: Four months. It's been about four months, doc. Ever since... well, ever since the garage fire. Four months doesn't sound like a long time when you just say it out loud, you know? Like, if you're talking about a commercial job, four months is nothing, you're barely past the framing and the electrical rough-in. But living with this? Every single day of these last four months has felt like a goddamn eternity. \n\nIt started right after the fire, obviously, but it really just... it locked in, it got so much heavier when my nephew started staying with us. Because, like I was saying before, my sister-in-law had to figure out what to do to keep them afloat. Four months of just... suffocating. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It's... it's both, doc. I mean, the guilt is always there, humming in the background like a bad transformer, but the actual fire? The memory of it? It doesn't just stay in the past. It comes back and it completely hijacks me. 

I'll be at work, right? Just doing my job, something I've done a million times. I'll be in the middle of wiring a main service panel, maybe just stripping a piece of copper wire, completely focused on making sure the connections are tight and the grounding is right. And then... I don't even know what triggers it exactly, but it's like the whole job site just drops out from under me. Literally, the floor, the walls of whatever basement or unfinished commercial space I'm in, they just vanish. 

And suddenly I'm right back there. I'm not at work anymore, I'm back in that garage. I can smell the smoke. God, that smell... it's not just like a campfire, you know? It's that thick, toxic, acrid smell of melting wire insulation and burning chemicals, and it fills up my nose and coats the back of my throat so intensely that I actually start coughing and choking on it in real life. And I see him. I see my brother right there in the flames, I see it all happening exactly the way it did, and I can't stop it. I can't reach him. I'm just trapped in that moment all over again.

And it's not even just at work where it happens, which is the crazy part. It happens at home, too. Like, my nephew... he'll be heading outside to mess around in the driveway or grab his bike, and I'll just be sitting in the living room trying to drink a cup of coffee, trying to have a normal afternoon. And he'll shut the garage door. Just a normal, everyday sound, right? Just the heavy thud of a door closing. But the second I hear that sound... boom. The living room is gone. I'm instantly back in the heat and the panic. It's so incredibly real. It's not just a bad memory where you're thinking about something sad, it's like I'm physically transported back to the worst day of my life, over and over again, and I just have to stand there and watch it happen while I'm breathing in smoke that no one else can smell.

I know circuits, I know loads, I know how to make things safe. That's my whole job, making sure things are safe and done by the book. \n\nBut... I can tell you about the morning, I can tell you we had coffee from that little gas station down on Route 9, I can tell you the sun was just barely coming up and my truck was running rough because I hadn't changed the spark plugs... it was just a Tuesday, a stupid, completely normal Tuesday. We were just supposed to be doing a standard job. \n\nBut when it comes to the actual... to the moment it went wrong... I just... my brain hits a wall. It's like a circuit breaker tripping, ironically enough. Just... *snap*, and everything goes dark and loud at the same time. I want to tell you, I know I'm supposed to tell you so you can figure this out and help me, but I literally can't make the words come out of my throat. It's just this massive knot of noise and... and guilt, just so much guilt. I just know I should have stopped it. I should have known better, I was the one with the training. But the actual thing that happened? The details of it? I can't... I can't even say it out loud. I've tried, I really have. I'll sit in my truck for hours after work just trying to say it to the steering wheel and I just... I can't do it. I just can't.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding places, people, or activities that remind you of what happened to your brother?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah. Yeah, I do. It's... it's gotten pretty bad, honestly. Take my own garage, for instance. I used to spend hours out there. I had a whole setup, you know? Workbench, pegboard with all my tools outlined so I knew exactly where everything was, a little mini-fridge for a couple beers on a Saturday afternoon. It was my sanctuary. But now? Now I don't go in there unless I absolutely, positively have to. Like, if the lawnmower is in there and the grass is a foot high and my wife is giving me that look, I'll go in, grab it, and get out as fast as I can. Just being in there, surrounded by all those tools, the smell of oil and sawdust... it just makes my chest tight. I can't breathe right.\n\nAnd it's not just the garage. I used to volunteer down at our church, you know? Just helping out with the electrical stuff. They've got this old building, beautiful but the wiring is a nightmare, a real patchwork job from the seventies. I was slowly going through, updating the panels, making sure it was up to code so, God forbid, nothing happened. But I had to quit. I just... I called the pastor and told him I didn't have the time anymore, which was a lie, but I couldn't face it. I couldn't look at another breaker box that wasn't for my actual paying job, and even those are hard enough. \n\nAnd people... man, people don't get it. They mean well, but they just don't get it. Guys at work or folks around the neighborhood, they'll start talking about, I don't know, restoring an old car, or they'll bring up... they'll bring up the fire. Just casually, like it's the weather or last night's game. And I just shut them down. Fast. I don't even try to be polite about it anymore. I'll just turn my back and walk away, or I'll change the subject so hard they get whiplash. I just can't hear about it. I can't hear about car projects, I can't hear about anything that even skirts around the edges of... of what happened. It's like if I let them talk, if I let myself be around any of those things, that whole knot of noise in my head is just going to explode. So, yeah, I avoid a lot. I avoid pretty much everything I can.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever experience memories of what happened to your brother that feel like they're happening all over again, like you're reliving them?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Yeah, all the time. It's... it's not even like a memory, doc, it's not like when you remember what you had for breakfast or, you know, a vacation you took years ago. It's... it's like I'm time-traveling, but not the fun kind you see in the movies. It's like my brain just yanks me backward. \n\nI'll just be at work, right? Middle of a Tuesday, perfectly normal day. I'll be in the middle of wiring a panel, stripping the casing off a 12-gauge wire, my hands doing what they've done ten thousand times before, and then... I don't know, maybe a truck backfires out on the street, or maybe someone drops a heavy wrench on the concrete. Or even at home, my nephew, he'll come over and he'll slam the heavy metal garage door shut, you know the way kids do, just yank it down so it hits the floor hard... and instantly, I'm not there anymore. The whole job site, or my driveway, it just drops right out from under me. \n\nSuddenly, I'm not holding my wire strippers. I'm right back in that moment. I can smell it. God, the smell of the smoke, it's so thick and acrid, it burns the back of my throat, like burning plastic and oil and... and I can see him. I can see my brother. I can see the whole thing happening right in front of me, in real time, and my heart starts hammering against my ribs so hard I think I'm going to have a heart attack right then and there. I'll start sweating, my hands will shake, and I'll actually yell out, like I'm trying to warn him, but I'm standing in an unfinished kitchen in a new subdivision, yelling at a drywaller who's staring at me like I've lost my mind. It takes me... I don't know, minutes, sometimes longer, just to realize where I actually am, to feel the floor under my boots again. It's terrifying, doc. It's like I'm never really safe from it, because it can just ambush me out of nowhere.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about what happened to your brother?\n\nPatient: Guilt? I... doc, guilt doesn't even begin to cover it. It's not just a passing thought, it's like a physical thing. There's this heavy, suffocating mix of guilt and anger just sitting right here in the middle of my chest, all day, every single day. From the minute I wake up and stare at the ceiling, before I even put my work boots on, it's there. I'll be driving to a job site, gripping the steering wheel, and I'm just so angry at myself, angry at how everything went down, and the guilt is just eating me alive from the inside out. \n\nBut it's the worst... God, it's the absolute worst when I look at my nephew. My brother's boy. He's over at the house a lot now, you know, because my sister-in-law is picking up extra shifts to keep the mortgage paid, so my wife and I watch him. And he's a great kid, he really is, he's got his dad's exact same smile, that same way of squinting when he's trying to figure out how a tool works. But every time I look at him across the kitchen table while he's doing his math homework, or when he asks me to help him fix the chain on his bike... it just destroys me. \n\nI look at this kid and all I can think is that he lost his dad. He lost his actual dad, the guy who was supposed to teach him to drive, the guy who was supposed to be there for everything. And instead? Instead he got stuck with me. He's stuck with the uncle who was supposed to protect his dad, the guy with the electrical training who should have stopped the whole thing from happening. \n\nIt's just so incredibly unfair to him, you know? I'm sitting there trying to be a surrogate father figure, trying to smile and be normal, and my chest is just screaming with this anger and this guilt. I'm angry my brother is gone, I'm angry that I'm the one who walked away, and I just feel so sick with guilt that this poor kid has to settle for me. I can't shake it, doc. It never lifts. It just sits there, heavy and dark, all day long. I don't know how to live with it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about harming yourself since your brother's accident?\n\nPatient: Harming myself... well... I mean, I haven't, like, sat down and written a note or planned anything out, if that's what you're asking. I don't have a specific plan to do anything. But... I guess, if I'm being completely honest with you, doc, I haven't exactly been keeping myself safe, either. It's... it's hard to explain. It's more like I just don't care anymore. \n\nLike, I've been driving way, way too fast between jobs lately. I'm usually in my heavy work truck, the bed is just loaded down with heavy tools and spools of wire, and I'll be out on the highway just burying the needle, going 90, 95 miles an hour, weaving in and out of lanes. I know it's incredibly stupid. Before all this, I was the guy who always drove the speed limit, the guy who always checked his blind spots twice because I wanted to get home to my wife. But now? Now I just press the gas pedal down to the floorboard, I watch the cars blur by, and I just don't feel anything. It's like I'm daring the road to take me out. \n\nAnd it's not just the driving, it's at work, too. As an electrician, safety is literally the first thing they teach you on day one of your apprenticeship. Lock out, tag out, always assume a circuit is live until you test it yourself with your meter. You respect the electricity or it kills you, it's that simple. But lately... man, I'll be working around live wires, main service panels with enough juice to stop your heart in a fraction of a second, and I just don't slow down like I used to. I don't double-check the breakers. I'll just reach right in there, stripping wires and making connections hot, completely ignoring every single safety rule I've followed for the last twenty years. My hands are right there next to the bare copper, and I know exactly what would happen if my pliers slipped or if I grounded out, but I just... I don't care. \n\nIt's almost like I don't care if something bad happens to me. If I wrap the truck around a bridge abutment on my way to a site, or if I take a lethal shock in some stranger's basement... part of me thinks that would just be it, you know? It would just be over. The guilt, the noise in my head, the waking up every day feeling like I'm suffocating... it would all just stop. So, no, I'm not actively holding a gun to my head, but I'm definitely living like a guy who wouldn't mind if the lights went out for good.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these feelings of guilt and anxiety since your brother's accident?\n\nPatient: Four months. It's been about four months, doc. Ever since... well, ever since the garage fire. Four months doesn't sound like a long time when you just say it out loud, you know? Like, if you're talking about a commercial job, four months is nothing, you're barely past the framing and the electrical rough-in. But living with this? Every single day of these last four months has felt like a goddamn eternity. \n\nIt started right after the fire, obviously, but it really just... it locked in, it got so much heavier when my nephew started staying with us. Because, like I was saying before, my sister-in-law had to figure out what to do to keep them afloat. The funeral expenses, the mortgage, all of it just dropped right on her shoulders overnight, so she started taking all those extra shifts. And suddenly the boy is just... he's at our house all the time. He's practically living with us. Every afternoon, every weekend. \n\nSo for four solid months, I wake up, I have this crushing weight of guilt sitting right on my chest, and then I walk out into my kitchen and there he is. Just sitting there eating cereal, looking exactly like his dad. And I can't escape it. I can't even just go home and try to clear my head after work because the living, breathing reminder of what I let happen is sitting right there at my kitchen table. Four months of just... suffocating. I keep thinking maybe one morning I'll wake up and it won't be so loud in my head, but it's just been one long, continuous nightmare since the day that garage burned. Honestly, I don't know how people live with this kind of thing for years. Just these four months alone feel like they've aged me ten years.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience flashbacks of the garage fire itself, or is it more the emotions and guilt associated with the event that you relive?\n\nPatient: It's... it's both, doc. I mean, the guilt is always there, humming in the background like a bad transformer, but the actual fire? The memory of it? It doesn't just stay in the past. It comes back and it completely hijacks me. \n\nI'll be at work, right? Just doing my job, something I've done a million times. I'll be in the middle of wiring a main service panel, maybe just stripping a piece of copper wire, completely focused on making sure the connections are tight and the grounding is right. And then... I don't even know what triggers it exactly, but it's like the whole job site just drops out from under me. Literally, the floor, the walls of whatever basement or unfinished commercial space I'm in, they just vanish. \n\nAnd suddenly I'm right back there. I'm not at work anymore, I'm back in that garage. I can smell the smoke. God, that smell... it's not just like a campfire, you know? It's that thick, toxic, acrid smell of melting wire insulation and burning chemicals, and it fills up my nose and coats the back of my throat so intensely that I actually start coughing and choking on it in real life. And I see him. I see my brother right there in the flames, I see it all happening exactly the way it did, and I can't stop it. I can't reach him. I'm just trapped in that moment all over again.\n\nAnd it's not even just at work where it happens, which is the crazy part. It happens at home, too. Like, my nephew... he'll be heading outside to mess around in the driveway or grab his bike, and I'll just be sitting in the living room trying to drink a cup of coffee, trying to have a normal afternoon. And he'll shut the garage door. Just a normal, everyday sound, right? Just the heavy thud of a door closing. But the second I hear that sound... boom. The living room is gone. I'm instantly back in the heat and the panic. It's so incredibly real. It's not just a bad memory where you're thinking about something sad, it's like I'm physically transported back to the worst day of my life, over and over again, and I just have to stand there and watch it happen while I'm breathing in smoke that no one else can smell.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

I don't double-check the breakers. I'll just reach right in there, stripping wires and making connections hot, completely ignoring every single safety rule I've followed for the last twenty years. My hands are right there next to the bare copper, and I know exactly what would happen if my pliers slipped or if I grounded out, but I just... I don't care. \n\nIt's almost like I don't care if something bad happens to me. If I wrap the truck around a bridge abutment on my way to a site, or if I take a lethal shock in some stranger's basement... part of me thinks that would just be it, you know? It would just be over. The guilt, the noise in my head, the waking up every day feeling like I'm suffocating... it would all just stop. So, no, I'm not actively holding a gun to my head, but I'm definitely living like a guy who wouldn't mind if the lights went out for good.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these feelings of guilt and anxiety since your brother's accident?\n\nPatient: Four months. It's been about four months, doc. Ever since... well, ever since the garage fire. Four months doesn't sound like a long time when you just say it out loud, you know? Like, if you're talking about a commercial job, four months is nothing, you're barely past the framing and the electrical rough-in. But living with this? Every single day of these last four months has felt like a goddamn eternity. \n\nIt started right after the fire, obviously, but it really just... it locked in, it got so much heavier when my nephew started staying with us. Because, like I was saying before, my sister-in-law had to figure out what to do to keep them afloat. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Avoid thinking about it? Doc, I try to avoid *everything* that even remotely points in that direction. It's not just the memories, it's... it's my whole life that I have to tiptoe around now to keep from thinking about it. 

Like, take my own house, for example. I have a two-car attached garage. Used to be my sanctuary, you know? I had my workbench set up perfectly, pegboard with all my tools outlined, a little fridge for beers, the whole nine yards. But now? I don't go in my own garage unless I absolutely, positively have to. I mean, I park my heavy work truck out in the driveway in the rain, the snow, whatever, just so I don't have to hit the clicker and see inside. If my wife asks me to get some boxes down, or if I need a specific socket wrench that I know is sitting right there in the top drawer of my red Craftsman box... I just can't do it. I'll literally drive down to Home Depot and buy a brand new wrench rather than walk through that door. Just the thought of standing on a concrete garage floor, surrounded by tools and oil stains... it makes my chest tighten up so bad I can't breathe. 

And it's spilled over into the rest of my life, too. I used to be the guy who helped out at our church with all their maintenance. They have this really old building, wiring from the dark ages, some old knob and tube stuff still in the walls, and I was always over there on weekends, volunteering my time, upgrading their panels, running new conduit, swapping out ballasts. It was just what I did. But after the accident... I completely quit helping with the church electrical stuff. The pastor called me a couple weeks ago because half the lights in the fellowship hall went out and they had a potluck coming up, and I just... I couldn't even give him a polite excuse. I just said, 'I'm done, you gotta call a commercial contractor.' I can't do the side-project thing anymore. It's too close to what my brother and I were doing that day. 

And then there's dealing with other people. You wouldn't believe how much guys talk about cars, or how nosy they get. If I'm at the electrical supply house picking up materials, or just grabbing a coffee at the gas station, and some guy starts talking about his weekend car project, or rebuilding an engine, or... or if someone actually has the nerve to bring up the fire and ask how I'm holding up? I shut them down fast. I don't care if I come off like a total jerk. I don't give them a polite nod, I don't let them finish their sentence. I just say, 'We are not talking about that,' and I walk away. I've left a full cart of wire and breakers sitting right there at the counter and just walked out to my truck. I just... I can't let my brain go there, doc. If I let them talk, if I picture what they're saying, it pulls me right back into the flames, so I just have to block it all out. I have to.
